"source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:22420]\n=== PDF page 1 ===\nHigh-throughput Generative Inference of Large Language Models\nwith a Single GPU\nYing Sheng 1 Lianmin Zheng 2 Binhang Yuan 3 Zhuohan Li 2 Max Ryabinin 4 5 Daniel Y. Fu 1 Zhiqiang Xie 1\nBeidi Chen 6 7 Clark Barrett 1 Joseph E. Gonzalez 2 Percy Liang 1 Christopher R´e 1 Ion Stoica 2 Ce Zhang 3\nAbstract\nThe high computational and memory requirements of large language model (LLM) inference traditionally make it\nfeasible only with multiple high-end accelerators. Motivated by the emerging demand for latency-insensitive tasks\nwith batched processing, this paper initiates the study of high-throughput LLM inference using limited resources,\nsuch as a single commodity GPU. We present FlexGen, a high-throughput generation engine for running LLMs\nwith limited GPU memory. FlexGen can be ﬂexibly conﬁgured under various hardware resource constraints by\naggregating memory and computation from the GPU, CPU, and disk. Through a linear programming optimizer,\nit searches for efﬁcient patterns to store and access tensors. FlexGen further compresses these weights and the\nattention cache to 4 bits with negligible accuracy loss. These techniques enable FlexGen to have a larger space of\nbatch size choices and thus signiﬁcantly increase maximum throughput. As a result, when running OPT-175B on\na single 16GB GPU, FlexGen achieves signiﬁcantly higher throughput compared to state-of-the-art ofﬂoading\nsystems, reaching a generation throughput of 1 token/s for the ﬁrst time with an effective batch size of 144. On\nthe HELM benchmark, FlexGen can benchmark a 30B model with a 16GB GPU on 7 representative sub-scenarios\nin 21 hours. The code is available at https://github.com/FMInference/FlexGen.\n1. Introduction\nIn recent years, large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance across a wide range of tasks (Brown\net al., 2020; Bommasani et al., 2021; Zhang et al., 2022; Chowdhery et al., 2022). Along with these unprecedented\ncapabilities, generative LLM inference comes with unique challenges. These models can have billions, if not trillions\nof parameters (Chowdhery et al., 2022; Fedus et al., 2022), which leads to extremely high computational and memory\nrequirements to run. For example, GPT-175B requires 325GB of GPU memory simply to load its model weights. Fitting\nthis model onto GPUs would require at least ﬁve A100 (80GB) GPUs and complex parallelism strategies (Pope et al., 2022;\nAminabadi et al., 2022). Thus, lowering LLM inference resource requirements has recently attracted intense interest.\nIn this paper, we focus on a setting that we call throughput-oriented generative inference. In addition to interactive use\ncases such as chatbots, LLMs are also applied to many “back-of-house” tasks such as benchmarking (Liang et al., 2022),\ninformation extraction (Narayan et al., 2018), data wrangling (Narayan et al., 2022), and form processing (Chen et al.,\n2021). One key characteristic of these tasks is that they often require running LLM inference in batches over a large number\nof tokens (e.g., all the documents in a company’s corpus), and are less sensitive to the latency of token generation. As a\nresult, it is possible to trade off latency for higher throughput in these workloads, providing opportunities to reduce resource\nrequirements.\nPrior efforts to lower resource requirements of LLM inference correspond to three directions: (1) model compression\nto decrease total memory footprint (Dettmers et al., 2022; Yao et al., 2022; Frantar et al., 2022; Xiao et al., 2022); (2)\ncollaborative inference to amortize inference cost via decentralization (Borzunov et al., 2022); and (3) ofﬂoading to utilize\nmemory from CPU and disk (Aminabadi et al., 2022; HuggingFace, 2022). These techniques have signiﬁcantly lowered the\nresource requirements for using LLMs, but there are distinct limitations. Research in the ﬁrst two directions often assume\nthat the model ﬁts into the GPU memory and thereby struggle to run 175B-scale models with a single commodity GPU. On\nthe other hand, state-of-the-art ofﬂoading-based systems in the third category do not achieve acceptable throughput on a\nsingle GPU due to inefﬁcient I/O scheduling and tensor placement. For example, these systems can be bottlenecked by\nsmall batch sizes (e.g., batch sizes of only one or two for OPT-175B in some cases).\n1Stanford University 2UC Berkeley 3ETH Zurich 4Yandex 5HSE University 6Meta 7Carnegie Mellon University.\nYing Sheng <ying1123@stanford.edu>, Lianmin Zheng <lian-\nminzheng@gmail.com>, Binhang Yuan <biyuan@inf.ethz.ch>, Zhuohan Li <zhuohan@cs.berkeley.edu>, Max Ryabinin <mryabinin0@gmail.com>, Daniel Y. Fu <danfu@cs.stanford.edu>,\nZhiqiang Xie <xiezhq@stanford.edu>, Beidi Chen <beidic@andrew.cmu.edu>, Clark Barrett <barrett@cs.stanford.edu>, Joseph E. Gonzalez <jegonzal@cs.berkeley.edu>, Percy Liang <pli-\nang@cs.stanford.edu>, Christopher R´e <chrismre@cs.stanford.edu>, Ion Stoica <istoica@cs.berkeley.edu>, Ce Zhang <ce.zhang@inf.ethz.ch>.\narXiv:2303.06865v1  [cs.LG]  13 Mar 2023\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\n210\n211\n212\n213\nLatency (s)\n20\n2\n2\n2\n4\n2\n6\n2\n8\nOPT-175B\nFlexGen (c)\nFlexGen\nDeepSpeed\nAccelerate\n28\n29\n210\nLatency (s)\n23\n21\n2\n1\n2\n3\nOPT-30B\nGeneration throughput (token/s)\nFigure 1. The latency and throughput trade-offs of three ofﬂoading-based systems for OPT-175B (left) and OPT-30B (right) on a single\nNVIDIA T4 (16 GB) GPU with 208 GB CPU DRAM. FlexGen achieves a new Pareto-optimal frontier with 100× higher maximum\nthroughput for OPT-175B. Other systems cannot further increase throughput due to out-of-memory issues. “(c)” denotes compression.\n16 GB\n208 GB\n1.5 TB\nGPU\nCPU\nDisk\n12 GB/s\n2 GB/s\nOur focus is designing efﬁcient ofﬂoading strategies for high-throughput generative inference, on a\nsingle commodity GPU. To run an LLM with limited GPU memory, we can ofﬂoad it to secondary\nstorage and perform computation part-by-part by partially loading it. On a typical machine, there\nare three levels of the memory hierarchy, as illustrated in the ﬁgure to the right. Higher levels are\nfaster but scarce, while lower levels are slower but abundant. In throughput-oriented scenarios, we\ncan sacriﬁce latency by using a large batch size, and amortize the expensive I/O operations among\ndifferent memory hierarchies over a large batch of inputs, overlapped with computation. Fig. 1 shows the latency-throughput\ntrade-off of three inference systems with ofﬂoading on a single NVIDIA T4 (16 GB) GPU. Note that the performance in\nterms of latency and throughput on limited resources is signiﬁcantly inferior to that of the cases with sufﬁcient resources.\nAchieving high-throughput generative inference with limited GPU memory is challenging even if we can sacriﬁce the\nlatency. The ﬁrst challenge is to design an efﬁcient ofﬂoading strategy. During generative inference, there are three kinds\nof tensors: weights, activations, and key-value (KV) cache. The strategy should specify what tensors to ofﬂoad, where to\nofﬂoad them within the three-level memory hierarchy, and when to ofﬂoad them during inference. The batch-by-batch,\ntoken-by-token, and layer-by-layer structure of the computation forms a complex dependency graph where there are multiple\nways to conduct computation. Together, these choices form a complex design space. Existing ofﬂoading-based inference\nsystems (Aminabadi et al., 2022; HuggingFace, 2022) inherit strategies from training, which turn out to be some suboptimal\npoints for inference, performing excessive I/O and achieving throughput far below theoretical hardware limits.\nThe second challenge is to develop effective compression strategies. Previous works have demonstrated promising results\nin compressing the weights and activations of LLMs. However, when combining compression with ofﬂoading for high-\nthroughput generative inference, the I/O costs and memory reduction of the weights and KV cache become more important,\nmotivating alternative compression schemes.\nTo address these challenges, we present FlexGen, an ofﬂoading framework for high-throughput LLM inference. FlexGen\naggregates memory from the GPU, CPU, and disk, and efﬁciently schedules I/O operations, along with possible compression\nmethods and distributed pipeline parallelism.\n(Contribution 1) We formally deﬁne a search space of possible ofﬂoading strategies by considering computation schedule,\ntensor placement, and computation delegation. We prove that our search space captures a computation order with I/O\ncomplexity within 2× of optimality. We then develop a linear programming-based search algorithm to optimize the\nthroughput within the search space. This algorithm can be conﬁgured for various hardware speciﬁcations and can be easily\nextended to incorporate latency and throughput constraints, thus helping to navigate the trade-off space smoothly. Compared\nwith existing strategies, our solution uniﬁes the placement of weights, activations, and the KV cache, enabling a dramatically\nhigher batch size upper bound, which is key to achieving high throughput.\n(Contribution 2) We show that it is possible to compress both the weights and KV cache for LLMs like OPT-175B to 4\nbits without retraining or calibration, all with negligible accuracy loss. This is achieved through ﬁne-grained group-wise\nquantization (Shen et al., 2020), which is suitable for reducing I/O costs and memory usage during ofﬂoading.\n(Contribution 3) We demonstrate the efﬁciency of FlexGen by running OPT-175B on NVIDIA T4 (16GB) GPUs. Compared\nto DeepSpeed Zero-Inference (Aminabadi et al., 2022) and Hugging Face Accelerate (HuggingFace, 2022), two state-of-the-\nart ofﬂoading-based inference systems, FlexGen often allows a batch size that is orders of magnitude larger. As a result,\nFlexGen can achieve much higher throughputs. On a single T4 GPU with 208 GB CPU DRAM and 1.5 TB SSD, input\n2\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nsequence length 512, and output sequence length 32:\n• With the same latency of 5000 seconds, FlexGen (effective batch size 64, or 2048 tokens in total) can achieve more\nthan 40× higher throughput than DeepSpeed Zero-Inference (batch size 1, or 32 tokens in total), while Hugging Face\nAccelerate cannot complete a single batch.\n• By allowing a higher latency of 12000 seconds, FlexGen achieves 69× higher maximum throughput compared to\nbaselines because it can enlarge the effective batch size to 256 (8192 tokens generated in total), while DeepSpeed\nZero-Inference and Hugging Face Accelerate cannot use a batch size larger than 2 due to out-of-memory issues.\n• If allowing 4-bit compression, FlexGen can reach 100× higher maximum throughput with effective batch size 144\n(4608 tokens generated in total) with latency 4000 seconds by holding all weights in CPU and getting rid of disk\nofﬂoading.\nWe also compare ofﬂoading and decentralized collective inference based on FlexGen and Petals (Borzunov et al., 2022) as\ntwo representative systems. We conduct comparisons between the two systems from the aspects of delay and bandwidth of\nthe decentralized network and output sequence length. The results show that FlexGen outperforms a decentralized Petals\ncluster in terms of per-GPU throughput and can even achieve lower latency in certain cases.\n2. Related Work\nGiven the recent advances of LLMs, LLM inference has become an important workload, encouraging active research from\nboth the system side and the algorithm side.\nRecent years have witnessed the emergence of systems specialized for LLM inference, such as FasterTransformer (NVIDIA,\n2022), Orca (Yu et al., 2022), LightSeq (Wang et al., 2021), PaLM inference (Pope et al., 2022), TurboTransformers (Fang\net al., 2021), DeepSpeed Inference (Aminabadi et al., 2022), and Hugging Face Accelerate (HuggingFace, 2022). Unfortu-\nnately, most of these systems focus on latency-oriented scenarios with high-end accelerators, limiting their deployment\nfor throughput-oriented inference on easily accessible hardware. To enable LLM inference on such commodity hardware,\nofﬂoading is an essential technique — as far as we know, among current systems, only DeepSpeed Zero-Inference and\nHugging Face Accelerate support ofﬂoading. These inference systems typically inherit the ofﬂoading techniques from\ntraining systems (Rajbhandari et al., 2021; Ren et al., 2021; Li et al., 2022; Huang et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2018) but ignore\nthe special computational property of generative inference. They fail to exploit the structure of the throughput-oriented\nLLM inference computation and miss great opportunities for efﬁcient scheduling of I/O trafﬁc. Another attempt to enable\nLLM inference on accessible hardware is collaborative computing proposed by Petals (Borzunov et al., 2022).\nBesides these advances on the system side, there are also many algorithm-oriented works that relax certain aspects of\ncomputation in LLM inference to accelerate the computation or reduce the memory footprint. Both sparsiﬁcation (Hoeﬂer\net al., 2021; Frantar & Alistarh, 2023) and quantization (Kwon et al., 2022; Yao et al., 2022; Park et al., 2022; Xiao et al.,\n2022; Frantar et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2022) have been adopted for LLM inference. On the quantization side, prior\nworks have shown weights can be compressed down to 3 bits without compressing activations (Frantar et al., 2022), or both\nweights and activations can be compressed to 8 bits (Yao et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2022; Xiao et al., 2022). In FlexGen,\nwe compress both the weights and KV cache to 4 bits and show how to combine the compression with ofﬂoading to make\nfurther improvements.\nWithin domains outside of LLM inference, memory optimizations and ofﬂoading have been studied for training (Huang\net al., 2020; Ren et al., 2021; Steiner et al., 2022) and linear algebra (Jia-Wei & Kung, 1981; Demmel, 2013).\n3. Background: LLM Inference\nIn this section, we describe the LLM inference workﬂow and its memory footprint.\nGenerative Inference. A typical LLM generative inference task consists of two stages: i) the preﬁll stage which takes a\nprompt sequence to generate the key-value cache (KV cache) for each transformer layer of the LLM; and ii) the decoding\nstage which utilizes and updates the KV cache to generate tokens step-by-step, where the current token generation depends\non previously generated tokens. Formally, the computation of a transformer layer can be summarized below:\nFor a particular inference computation, denote the batch size by b, the input sequence length by s, the output sequence\nlength by n, the hidden dimension of the transformer by h1, the hidden dimension of the second MLP layer by h2, and the\n3\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\ntotal number of transformer layers by l. Given the weight matrices of a transformer layer speciﬁed by wi\nK, wi\nQ, wi\nV , wi\nO,\nwi\n1, wi\n2, where wi\nK, wi\nQ, wi\nV , wi\nO ∈Rh1×h1, w1 ∈Rh1×h2, and w2 ∈Rh2×h1.\nDuring the preﬁll phase, the input of the i-th layer is speciﬁed by xi, and key, value, query, and output of the\nattention layer is speciﬁed by xi\nK, xi\nV , xi\nQ, xi\nOut, where xi, xi\nK, xi\nV , xi\nQ, xi\nOut ∈Rb×s×h1. Then, the cached key, value\ncan be computed by:\nxi\nK = xi · wi\nK;\nxi\nV = xi · wi\nV\nThe rest of the computation in the i-th layer is:\nxi\nQ = xi · wi\nQ\nxi\nOut = fSoftmax\n\u0012\nxi\nQxi\nK\nT\n√\nh\n\u0013\n· xi\nV · wi\nO + xi\nxi+1 = frelu\n\u0000xi\nOut · w1\n\u0001\n· w2 + xi\nOut\nDuring the decoding phase, given the embedding of the current generated token in the i-th layer noted by ti ∈Rb×1×h1,\nthe inference computation needs to i) update the KV cache; and ii) compute the output of the current layer. The update of\nthe KV cache can be formalized as:\nxi\nK ←Concat\n\u0000xi\nK, ti · wi\nK\n\u0001\nxi\nV ←Concat\n\u0000xi\nV , ti · wi\nV\n\u0001\nThe rest of the computation for the current layer is:\nti\nQ = ti · wi\nQ\nti\nOut = fSoftmax\n\u0012\nti\nQxi\nK\nT\n√\nh\n\u0013\n· xi\nV · wi\nO + ti\nti+1 = frelu\n\u0000ti\nOut · w1\n\u0001\n· w2 + ti\nOut\nMemory Analysis. The memory footprint of LLM inference mainly comes from two components: the model weights and\nthe KV cache. Considering the OPT-175B model in FP16, the total number of bytes to store the parameters can be roughly\n1 calculated by l(8h2\n1 + 4h1h2). The total number of bytes to store the KV cache in peak is 4 × blh1(s + n).\nIn a realistic setting with a sufﬁcient number of GPUs, the OPT-175B model (l = 96, h1 = 12288, h2 = 49152) takes 325\nGB. With a batch size of b = 512, an input sequence length s = 512, and an output sequence length of n = 32, the total\nmemory required to store the KV cache is 1.2 TB, which is 3.8× the model weights, making the KV cache a new bottleneck\nof large-batch high-throughput inference. In the single GPU setting, the batch size is usually smaller but is still a signiﬁcant\nbottleneck. In FlexGen, for OPT-175B, we enlarge the effective batch size to 256 to achieve the throughput at 0.69 token/s.\nThroughput and Latency. Considering an effective batch size b, an input sequence length s, and an output sequence length\nof n, the latency t is deﬁned as the total number of seconds spent to process the prompts and generate all the bn tokens. The\ngeneration throughput is deﬁned as bn/t token/s.\nToken 0\nToken 1\nToken 2\nlayer\nDataset\n(infinite)\n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nbatch\nFigure 2. Computational graph of LLM inference.\n4. Ofﬂoading Strategy\nIn this section, we do not relax any computation of LLM inference and illustrate how to formalize the ofﬂoading procedure\nunder the GPU, CPU, and disk memory hierarchy. We ﬁrst formulate the problem and then construct the search space of\nthe possible ofﬂoading strategies in FlexGen. To ﬁnd an efﬁcient strategy, FlexGen builds an analytical cost model and\nsearches for conﬁgurations with an optimizer based on linear programming. We also show how to extend FlexGen to support\nmulti-GPU settings.\n1We ignore the word embedding layer(s), which is relatively small compared with all the transformer layers.\n4\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\nToken 0\nToken 1\nToken 2\nlayer\nDataset\n(infinite)\n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nbatch\n(a) Row-by-row schedule\nDataset\n(infinite)\n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nbatch\nblock\n(b) Zig-zag block schedule\nFigure 3. Two different schedules. The red arrows denote the\ncomputation order.\nfor i = 1 to generation length do\nfor j = 1 to num layers do\n// Compute a block with multiple GPU batches\nfor k = 1 to num GPU batches do\n// Load the weight of the next layer\nload weight(i, j + 1, k)\n// Store the cache and activation of the prev batch\nstore activation(i, j, k −1)\nstore cache(i, j, k −1)\n// Load the cache and activation of the next batch\nload cache(i, j, k + 1)\nload activation(i, j, k + 1)\n// Compute this batch\ncompute(i, j, k)\n// Synchronize all devices\nsynchronize()\nend for\nend for\nend for\nFigure 4. Block schedule with overlapping.\n4.1. Problem Formulation\nConsider a machine with three devices: a GPU, a CPU, and a disk. The GPU and CPU can perform computation while the\ndisk cannot. The three devices form a three-level memory hierarchy where the GPU has the smallest but fastest memory and\nthe disk has the largest but slowest memory. When an LLM cannot ﬁt entirely within the GPU, we need to ofﬂoad it to\nsecondary storage and perform computation part-by-part by partially loading the LLM.\nWe formulate the generative inference with ofﬂoading as a graph traversal problem. Fig. 2 shows an example computational\ngraph, where the model has 4 layers and we generate 3 tokens per prompt. As our focus is throughput-oriented scenarios,\nwe assume a given dataset with an inﬁnite number of prompts that need to be processed. In the ﬁgure, a square means the\ncomputation of a batch on a layer on the GPU. The squares with the same color share the same layer weights. We deﬁne a\nvalid path as a path that traverses (i.e., computes) all squares, while subject to the following constraints:\n• A square can only be computed if all squares to its left on the same row were computed.\n• To compute a square on a device, all its inputs (weights, activations, cache) must be loaded to the same device.\n• After being computed, a square produces two outputs: activations and KV cache. The activations should be stored until\nits right sibling is computed. The KV cache should be stored until the rightmost square on the same row is computed.\n• At any time, the total size of tensors stored on a device cannot exceed its memory capacity.\nThe goal is to ﬁnd a valid path that minimizes the total execution time, which includes the compute cost and I/O cost when\nmoving tensors between devices.\n4.2. Search Space\nGiven the formulation above, we construct a search space for possible valid strategies in FlexGen.\nCompute schedule. Intuitively, there are two orders to traverse the graph in Fig. 2: row-by-row and column-by-column. All\nexisting systems (Aminabadi et al., 2022; HuggingFace, 2022) traverse the graph row-by-row, as shown in Fig. 3(a). This is\nreasonable because it is the fastest way to ﬁnish the generation for one batch and the KV cache can be freed immediately\nafter a row. However, because every two contiguous squares do not share weights, this schedule has to repeatedly load the\nweights and incurs huge I/O costs.\nTo reduce the I/O costs of the weights, we can traverse the graph column-by-column. All squares in a column share weights,\nso we can let the weights stay on GPU for reusing and only load/unload the activations and KV cache. However, we cannot\ntraverse a column all the way to the end because the activations and KV cache still need to be stored. Hence, we have to stop\nwhen they ﬁll the CPU and disk memory. Taking all this into consideration, we converge to a zig-zag block schedule, as\nshown in Fig. 3(b). Besides the zig-zag block schedule, there exists another more advanced and I/O-optimal schedule. We\nonly implement the simpler block schedule due to the practical implementation difﬁculty of the optimal schedule. However,\nwe prove that the block schedule is at most twice worse than the optimal schedule in Appendix A.2.\n5\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nTheorem 4.1. The I/O complexity of the zig-zag block schedule is within 2× of the optimal solution.\nAnother typical optimization is overlapping. We can overlap the weights load of the next layer, cache/activation load of the\nnext batch, cache/activation store of the previous batch, and the computation of the current batch. Adding overlapping to\nthe block schedule results in Fig. 4. The ﬁrst six functions in the innermost loop can be seen as launched in parallel with\nsix logical threads because there are no dependencies. The last function then synchronizes these six logica\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 24000:40000]\nation of attention scores during decoding is I/O-bounded. Consider a case where the\nKV cache is stored on the CPU. Computing the attention scores on the GPU requires moving the entire KV cache to the\nGPU, which incurs a substantial I/O cost as the KV cache is huge. In contrast, computing the attention score on the CPU\ndoes not require moving the KV cache. It only requires moving the activations from the GPU to the CPU. Quantitatively,\nlet b be the GPU batch size, s be the sequence length, and h1 be the hidden size. The size of the moved KV cache is\nb × s × h1 × 4 bytes, and the size of the moved activation is b × h1 × 4 bytes, so computing attention score on CPU reduces\nI/O by s×. For long sequences (e.g., s ≥512), it is better to compute the attention scores on the CPU if the associated KV\ncache is stored on the CPU or disk.\n4.3. Cost Model and Policy Search\nThe schedule and placement in Section 4.2 constructs a search space with several parameters. Now we develop an analytical\ncost model to estimate the execution time given these algorithm parameters and hardware speciﬁcations.\nCost Model. The cost model predicts the latency during preﬁll for one layer denoted as Tpre, and the averaged latency\nduring decoding for one layer denoted as Tgen in one block. The total latency for computing a block can then be estimated\nas T = Tpre · l + Tgen · (n −1) · l, where l is the number of layers and n is the number of tokens to generate.\nAssuming perfect overlapping, Tpre can be estimated as Tpre = max(ctogp, gtocp, dtocp, ctodp, compp), where ctogp,\ngtocp, dtocp, ctodp, compp denote the latency of read from CPU to GPU, write from GPU to CPU, read from disk to CPU,\nwrite from CPU to disk, computation, respectively, during preﬁll for one layer.\nSimilarly, Tgen can be estimated as Tgen = max(ctogg, gtocg, dtocg, ctodg, compg), with ctogg, gtocg, dtocg, ctodg,\ncompg denoting the latency of read from CPU to GPU, write from GPU to CPU, read from disk to CPU, write from CPU to\ndisk, computation, respectively, during decoding for one layer.\nFor I/O terms like dtocg, it is estimated by summing up the I/O events, which contain weights, activations, and cache reads.\nThe size of FP16 weights for one transformer layer is 8h2\n1 + 4h1 · h2 bytes, with h1 denoting the hidden size, and h2\ndenoting the hidden size of the second MLP layer. Let bls be the block size and s be the prompt length; then the size of\nactivations for one layer is 2 · bls · h1. The size of the KV cache for one layer on average is 4 · bls · (s + n\n2 ) · h1. We have to\nload wd, hd, cd percent of weights, activations, and the KV cache from the disk respectively so that the total latency of disk\nread is dtocg =\n1\ndisk to cpu bandwidth((8h2\n1 + 4h1 · h2) · wd + 4 · bls · (s + n\n2 ) · h1 · cd + 2 · bls · h1 · hd).\nSimilarly for computation terms, we sum up all computation events, including matrix multiplications and batched matrix\nmultiplications on the CPU and the GPU.\nBesides latency estimation, we also estimate the peak memory usage of the GPU, CPU, and disk, and then we add memory\nconstraints. The full cost model is in Appendix A.3.\n6\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nPolicy Search. A policy includes 11 variables: block size bls, GPU batch size gbs, weight placement wg, wc, wd, activation\nplacement hg, hc, hd, and KV cache placement cg, cc, cd. In practice, the percentage cannot be an arbitrary real number\nbetween 0 and 1, because the tensor cannot be split arbitrarily. However, we relax the percentage variables in the cost model\nto be any real number between 0 and 1 since it is changing gradually. We solve the problem as a two-level optimization\nproblem. We ﬁrst enumerate a few choices of (bls, gbs) tuple. Typically, gbs is a multiple of 4, and bls is less than 20 so there\nare not too many choices. Then with the ﬁxed bls, gbs, ﬁnding the best placement p = (wg, wc, wd, cg, cc, cd, hg, hc, hd)\nbecomes a linear programming problem shown in Eq. (1). The linear programming problem can be solved very quickly\nbecause there are only 9 variables. This formulation can also be ﬂexibly extended to include latency constraints and model\napproximate methods such as compression.\nmin\np\nT/bls\ns.t.\ngpu peak memory\n<\ngpu mem capacity\ncpu peak memory\n<\ncpu mem capacity\ndisk peak memory\n<\ndisk mem capacity\nwg + wc + wd\n=\n1\ncg + cc + cd\n=\n1\nhg + hc + hd\n=\n1\n(1)\nTo use the cost model, we run proﬁling on the hardware to sample some data points and ﬁt the hardware parameters. We\nthen call the optimizer to get an ofﬂoading policy. Due to our relaxation and the hardness of accurately modeling peak\nmemory usage (e.g., fragmentation), sometimes a strategy from the policy search can run out of memory. In this case, we\nmanually adjust the policy slightly. The cost model can usually return a good policy, but it is common that a better policy\ncan be obtained by tuning manually.\n4.4. Extension to Multiple GPUs\nWe discuss how to extend the ofﬂoading strategy in FlexGen if there are multiple GPUs. Although we can ﬁnd a nearly\noptimal strategy for one GPU, the strategy is still heavily limited by I/O and has a low GPU utilization. If we are given more\nGPUs and more CPUs, model parallelism can be utilized to reduce the memory pressure of each GPU, which can potentially\nlead to a super-linear scaling in decoding.\nThere are two kinds of model parallelisms: tensor parallelism and pipeline parallelism (Narayanan et al., 2021; Zheng\net al., 2022). Tensor parallelism can reduce the single-query latency but pipeline parallelism can achieve good scaling on\nthroughput due to its low communication costs. Since we target throughput, FlexGen implements pipeline parallelism.\nWe use pipeline parallelism by equally partitioning an l-layer LLM on m GPUs, and then the execution of all GPUs follows\nthe same pattern. The problem is reduced to running an n/m-layer transformer on one GPU. We can directly reuse the\npolicy search developed for one GPU. To achieve micro-batch pipelining, a new for-loop is added to Fig. 4 to combine the\niteration-level pipeline parallel execution schedule (Huang et al., 2019; Yu et al., 2022) with our single-device ofﬂoading\nruntime.\n5. Approximate Methods\nThe previous section focuses on the exact computation. However, the inference throughput can be greatly boosted with\nnegligible accuracy loss by allowing some approximation, because LLMs are typically robust to careful approximations.\nThis section introduces two such approximations: group-wise quantization and sparse attention.\nGroup-wise Quantization. We show that both the weights and KV cache can be directly quantized into 4-bit integers\nwithout any retraining or calibration on OPT-175B, all while preserving similar accuracy (Section 6.2). When compared to\nsome related works (Yao et al., 2022; Dettmers et al., 2022; Xiao et al., 2022) that try to use integer matrix multiplication\nmainly for accelerated computation, the goal of quantization in our case is primarily for compression and reducing I/O costs.\nTherefore, we can choose a ﬁne-grained quantization format in favor of a high compression ratio and dequantize the tensors\nback to FP16 before computation. We use a ﬁne-grained group-wise asymmetric quantization method (Shen et al., 2020).\nGiven a tensor, we choose g contiguous elements along a certain dimension as a group. For each group, we compute the min\nand max of the group elements and quantize each element x into b-bit integers by xquant = round\n\u0010\nx−min\nmax−min × (2b −1)\n\u0011\n.\n7\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\nTable 1. Hardware Specs\nDevice\nModel\nMemory\nGPU\nNVIDIA T4\n16 GB\nCPU\nIntel Xeon @ 2.00GHz\n208 GB\nDisk\nCloud default SSD (NVMe)\n1.5 TB\nThe tensors are stored in the quantized format and converted back to FP16 before computation. Since both the weights and\nKV cache consume a signiﬁcant amount of memory, we compress both to 4 bits with a group size of 64. There are multiple\nways to choose which dimension to group on. We ﬁnd that grouping the weights along the output channel dimension and the\nKV cache along the hidden dimension preserves the accuracy while being runtime-efﬁcient in practice. One thing to mention\nis that such a ﬁne-grained group-wise quantization in FlexGen causes some overhead in compression and decompression.\nSuch an overhead could be very signiﬁcant if run on a CPU which makes the CPU delegation useless, so we turn off the CPU\ndelegation when enabling quantization. A concurrent work (Dettmers & Zettlemoyer, 2022) also ﬁnds that 4-bit precision\nis almost optimal for total model bits and zero-shot accuracy on OPT models. Compared to this previous work, we ﬁrst\npropose to compress the KV cache and present the results on OPT-175B.\nSparse Attention. We demonstrate that the sparsity of self-attention can be exploited by only loading the top 10% attention\nvalue cache on OPT-175B, all while maintaining the model quality. We present one simple Top-K sparse approximation.\nAfter computing the attention matrices, for each query, we calculate the indices of its Top-K tokens from the K cache. We\nthen simply drop the other tokens and only load a subset of the V cache according to the indices.\nThe application of these approximations is straightforward. We present these preliminary but interesting results and intend\nto emphasize that FlexGen is a general framework that can seamlessly plug in many approximation methods.\n6. Evaluation\nHardware. We run experiments on the NVIDIA T4 GPU instances from Google Cloud. The hardware speciﬁcations are\nlisted in Table 1. The read bandwidth of SSD is about 2GB/s and the write bandwidth is about 1GB/s.\nModel. OPT models (Zhang et al., 2022) with 6.7B to 175B parameters are used in the evaluation. Although we do not\nevaluate other models, the ofﬂoading in FlexGen can be applied to other transformer LLMs, e.g., GPT-3 (Brown et al.,\n2020), PaLM (Chowdhery et al., 2022), and BLOOM (Scao et al., 2022) because they all share a similar structure.\nWorkload. Our focus is high-throughput generation on a given dataset. We use synthetic datasets where all prompts are\npadded to the same length. The system is required to generate 32 tokens for each prompt. We test two prompt lengths: 512\nand 1024 (for experiments in more settings, see Appendix A.4). The evaluation metric is generation throughput, deﬁned as\nthe number of generated tokens / (preﬁll time + decoding time). Sometimes even running a full batch takes too long for\ncertain systems — in this cases, we generate fewer tokens and project the ﬁnal throughput. We use dummy model weights\nin throughput benchmarks for FlexGen and all baselines, but we use real weights for accuracy evaluations.\nBaseline. We use DeepSpeed ZeRO-Inference (Aminabadi et al., 2022) and Hugging Face Accelerate (HuggingFace,\n2022) as baselines. They are the only systems that can run LLMs with ofﬂoading when there is not enough GPU memory.\nDeepSpeed supports ofﬂoading the whole weights to the CPU or disk. It uses ZeRO data parallelism if there are multiple\nGPUs. Accelerate supports ofﬂoading a fraction of the weights. It does not support distributed GPUs on different machines.\nBoth of them use the row-by-row schedule and can only put cache/activations on GPU. These systems support different\nquantization methods. However, the quantization in Accelerate is not compatible with ofﬂoading, and the quantization in\nDeepSpeed cannot preserve accuracy up to 175B, so we do not enable quantization on these systems.\nImplementation. FlexGen is implemented on top of PyTorch (Paszke et al., 2019). FlexGen manages multiple CUDA\nstreams and CPU threads to overlap I/O with compute. FlexGen creates ﬁles for tensors stored on the disk and maps them as\nvirtual memory to access them.\n6.1. Ofﬂoading\nMaximum throughput benchmark. We ﬁrst evaluate the maximum generation throughput the systems can achieve with\none GPU on two prompt lengths. As shown in Table 2, FlexGen outperforms all baselines in all cases. On OPT-6.7B,\nAccelerate and FlexGen can successfully ﬁt the whole model into a single GPU, so they choose to only use the GPU.\nDeepSpeed has a higher memory overhead and cannot ﬁt OPT-6.7B into the GPU, so it uses slower CPU ofﬂoading. DeepSpeed and Accelerate store the KV cache on the GPU, so they cannot\nuse a very large batch size, while FlexGen ofﬂoads most weights and all KV cache to the CPU and enables a larger GPU\nbatch size. In addition, FlexGen reuses the weights by block scheduling. On OPT-175B, all systems start to ofﬂoad the\nweights to the disk. Baseline systems can only use a maximum batch size of 2, but FlexGen can use a GPU batch size of 32\nand a block size of 32 × 8, achieving a 69× higher throughput. With compression enabled, FlexGen achieves a 112× higher\ngeneration throughput on a single GPU for prompt sequence length 512. This huge improvement is because FlexGen uses an\neffective batch size of 144 and compresses the weights and KV cache to ﬁt into CPU memory to avoid slow disk swapping.\nMore details on the policy setups and effective batch sizes can be found in Appendix A.4. We also include Petals (Borzunov\net al., 2022) as an additional baseline. Petals uses 1 x T4 for OPT-6.7B, 4 x T4 for OPT-30B, and 24 x T4 for OPT-175B, but\nreports per-GPU throughput. The models are run in INT8 as the default for petals. We benchmark it under a good network\nassumption with a delay of less than 5ms and bandwidth of 1 Gbps. For a more comprehensive comparison with Petals, see\nSection 6.3.\nTable 3 shows the results on 4 machines with one GPU on each machine. Even with 4 GPUs, OPT-30B or OPT-175B still\ncannot ﬁt into these GPUs. Naively, we can run 4 independent FlexGen in a data-parallel fashion to get a linear scaling\non throughput. But here we show that pipeline parallelism can achieve super-linear scaling on decoding throughput. With\npipeline parallelism, the memory pressure of each machine is reduced so we can switch from small batch sizes to larger\nbatch sizes, or switch from disk ofﬂoading to CPU-only ofﬂoading. In Table 3, FlexGen does not achieve linear scaling on\ngeneration throughput (which counts both preﬁll and decoding time costs). This is because there are pipeline bubbles during\nthe preﬁll stage and our workload settings only generate 32 tokens. However, FlexGen achieves super-linear scaling on\ndecoding throughput (which only counts decoding time costs assuming the preﬁll is done). This means if we generate more\ntokens, pipeline parallelism will show its beneﬁts as decoding time will dominate.\nLatency-throughput trade-off. We conﬁgure these systems to achieve maximum throughput under various latency\nconstraints and draw their latency-throughput trade-off curves in Fig. 1. FlexGen sets a new Pareto-optimal frontier that\nsigniﬁcantly outperforms baselines. On the low-latency side, FlexGen supports partial ofﬂoading and uses more space for\nweights. On the \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 60000:80000]\nsecond column for bls\nsamples, until the last column for bls samples. We call bls as the block size as introduced in Section 4.2. The computed\nbls · n · l squares are called a block.\nAssume FP16 precision, to generate n · bls tokens during one block computation, we have to load n times the whole model\nweights, do I/O operations on activations with 2(2h1 · s · bls · l + 2h1 · bls · l · (n −1)) bytes in total, and do I/O on the KV\ncache with 4h1 · bls · l · (s · n + n(n −1)/2) bytes in total.\nLet w denote the size of one-layer weights. The peak memory used to store the weights, activations, and KV caches can be\nestimated as\npeak mem = w + 2h1 · bls + 4h1 · bls · l · (s + n)\nIf we only swap with CPU, then there is the constraint that peak mem < CPU memory - some overhead. Let cmem denote\nthe right hand, there is\nbls ≤\ncmem −w\n2h1 + 4h1 · l · (s + n) = bls1\nNow we show that there is a better schedule that gives the same I/O efﬁciency but can enlarge the bls by around 2 in some\ncases.\n15\n\n\n=== PDF page 16 ===\nToken 0\nToken 1\nToken 2\nlayer\nDataset\n(infinite)\n \n \n \n \n \n \n \n \nbatch\nToken 2\n \n \n \n \nFigure 6. diagonal block schedule\nDiagonal block schedule\nFigure 6 is an illustration of our diagonal block schedule. We have a block containing 4 GPU\nbatches, and we are going to generate 4 tokens with a model that has 4 layers. There will be a one-time warm-up phase\n(gray area) to compute the area above the diagonal. Then for each iteration, the system will compute a diagonal that contains\n4 sub-diagonals (4 squares enclosed by red outlines as the ﬁrst sub-diagonal, then 4 squares enclosed by blue outlines as the\nsecond sub-diagonal). After ﬁnishing the 4 sub-diagonals, it will repeat the same computation in the next row.\nFor simplicity, consider the good case that the memory capacity is large enough that the diagonal can cover all n generation\niterations for n tokens. The block size bls now is deﬁned as the number of samples touched by the diagonal.\nIn total, to compute one diagonal, the weights of each layer will be loaded once, and the I/O of the activations and KV cache\nwill be in size roughly as 1/n as the value in the zig-zag block schedule. There will be bls tokens generated. So the I/O per\ntoken is the same with the zig-zag block schedule after the one-time warm-up if for the same bls.\nThe peak memory needed to hold the necessary weights, activations, and KV cache is estimated as\npeak mem = w + 2h1 · bls + 4h1 · bls · l(2s + n)(n −1)\n2n\nfrom peak mem ≤cmem, we have\nbls ≤\nn(cmem −w)\n2h1 · n + 2h1 · l · (2s + n)(n −1) = bls2\nDespite a one-time warm-up at the beginning. The diagonal block schedule can accommodate a larger block size than\nzig-zag block schedule at the ratio of\nbls2\nbls1\n= 2s + 2n\n2s + n + O\n\u0012 1\nn\n\u0013\nwhich is close to 2 when n ≫s, and close to 1 when s ≫n.\nA larger bls does not change the activations and KV caches I/O per token, but can reduce the weights I/O per token\nproportionally, while weights I/O can normally occupy a large portion.\nDiscussions. In ofﬂoading setting, I/O is a signiﬁcant bottleneck in latency and throughput, so the diagonal block schedule\nshould be able to give considerable gain when n is relatively large compared to s and the memory is sufﬁciently large to ﬁt\nn samples.\nWhen the compute resources are sufﬁcient to avoid ofﬂoading, the diagonal block schedule can still help to reduce the peak\nmemory and enlarge the batch size, which increases GPU utilization.\nAnother beneﬁt compared to the zig-zag block schedule is that with the same throughput, the generation latency for each\nprompt is reduced. For example, suppose in the zig-zag block schedule the bls samples ﬁnish the generation at the same\ntime with latency T. In the diagonal block schedule, the ﬁrst bls/n samples ﬁnish the generation with latency T/n, the\nsecond bls/n samples ﬁnish with latency 2T/n, and so on. The average latency of completion is reduced by half.\nDespite its advantages, there are some difﬁculties in implementing the diagonal block schedule. The major implementation\ndifﬁculty is the dynamic update of the KV cache buffer. To improve runtime efﬁciency, FlexGen now pre-allocates\ncontinuous buffers for all KV cache at the beginning of a block. This works well for the zig-zag block schedule. However,\nfor the diagonal block schedule, pre-allocating continuous buffers make it impossible to save memory anymore. To utilize\n16\n\n\n=== PDF page 17 ===\nthe memory-saving property of the diagonal block schedule, one needs to implement efﬁcient attention computation on\nnon-contiguous memory.\nA.2.2. PROOF OF THEOREM 4.1\nNote that in any case when we move from computing a square to another square, we need to ofﬂoad and load the\ncorresponding KV cache. So that the total I/O incurred by KV cache is constant. The total I/O incurred by activations could\nvary, but despite the preﬁll phase, its size for each square is much smaller than the KV cache for the same square. In total,\nthe size of activations is around 1/(2s + n) of the size of KV cache. We will ignore the I/O incurred by activations for\nsimplicity, which can cause a multiplicative error of 1/(2s + n) at most. Then the only thing left is the weights I/O. Starting\nfrom now, the I/O complexity in the context refers to the I/O complexity incurred by weights.\nDeﬁnition A.1. We deﬁne the working state at any time when the GPU is computing a square as follows. Suppose there\nare k GPU batches working in progress. The column indices of the last squares that have been computed (including the\ncurrent one) are a1, a2, ..., ak, and 1 ≤ai ≤n × l. Different batches are identically independent, so w.l.o.g., suppose\na1 ≥a2 ≥... ≥ak. Then the working state is a tuple (a1, a2, ..., ak). A move that does a computation on a square is a pair\nof states s(1), s(2) that means transit from state s(1) to s(2).\nConsider an optimal order denoted as an inﬁnite sequence m1, m2, ...., m∞, where mi is the ith move. For each i, let si be\nthe current working state.\nLemma A.2. If there is a list of moves that start from state s, and back to state s at the end, the number of computed squares\nfor every column (one layer for one token) is the same.\nProof. Suppose the start state s = (a1, a2, ..., ak). For computations that occupy the whole row, the number of computed\nsquares for every column is the same. So we only need to consider the rows that have not been fully traversed (captured by\nthe end state). For each ai, if the underlying row has not been ﬁnished at the end, and ends with the index bi, then we pair ai\nwith bi. If the underlying row has been ﬁnished, we pair it with a newly opened but not ﬁnished row, still, let bi denote the\nnew index.\nThus we have transited from state Sa = (a1, a2, ..., ak) to another state Sb = (b1, b2, ..., bk). The indices in Sa are sorted\nby a1 ≥a2 ≥... ≥ak. The indices in Sb are not sorted, but bi is paired to ai according to the above paragraph. For each\ni, if bi > ai, we need to count the squares in (ai, bi] by 1. If bi < ai, we need to count the squares in (bi, ai] by -1. Now\nwe argue that for each column index j and 1 ≤j ≤n × l, the count over it is summed to 0. Suppose not, that there are p\npositive count and q negative count and p ̸= q. Then there are p values lower than j in state a and q values lower than j in\nstate b. This contradicts the fact that Sa and Sb are the same state with different orders. Therefore, the number of computed\nsquares for every column is the same.\nTheorem A.3. The diagonal block schedule is I/O-optimal asymptotically.\nProof. Notice that since the memory capacity is ﬁnite, the length of the state is ﬁnite, thus the number of the possible state\nis ﬁnite. If each state appears ﬁnite times in the sequence, then the sequence cannot be inﬁnite. Therefore, there exists a\nstate s that appears in the sequence inﬁnite times.\nLet j1, j2, ..., j∞be the indices in the sequence that have state s. The moves between each two neighboring s states\ncorrespond to a throughput. The moves between j1 and j2 should create the highest possible throughput that pushes from\nstate s to s. Otherwise, we can replace it to get a higher total throughput, which contradicts to that it is an optimal order. So\nthat we can repeat such a strategy between each neighboring ji, ji+1 to get an optimal compute order.\nNow the problem is reduced to ﬁnding an optimal compute order between j1 and j2. With inﬁnite loops, the highest\nthroughput from j1 to j2 gives the highest throughput among the whole sequence.\nAssume an optimal compute order between j1 and j2. From Lemma A.2, there is the same number of squares to be computed\nfor every column denoted as c. With such ﬁxed c, the throughput is determined by the I/O time between j1 and j2. The\nnumber of times we load weights for each color in Figure 2 determines the total I/O time. Each time we load weights, for\nexample, the weights for computing the yellow squares, we cannot compute two yellow squares in the same row without\nother weights swaps, because the squares between them have not been computed and require other weights.\n17\n\n\n=== PDF page 18 ===\nTherefore, for one load, we can only compute squares from different rows, which means all the caches and activations\ncorresponding to those squares need to be held (either on the CPU or on the disk). Every square corresponds to some\nmemory consumption, for example, the squares in the range of the i-th token cost caches for s + i −1 tokens. The sum\nof the memory consumption of all squares is a constant denoted as M. Let M ′ denote the memory capacity. The number\nof weights loading times is at least ⌈M/M ′⌉. Let tw denote the I/O time for loading weights for one color, the optimal\nthroughput is at most c/⌈M/M ′⌉/tw.\nIn the diagonal block schedule, after warm-up, each time with the loaded weights, the peak memory is the sum of the\nmemory consumption of each computed square, which is the same each time we load weights. We can set it to hit M ′2.\nTake c number of diagonals as the repeated list of moves denoted as ⃗q. Set the starting state to be s mentioned before, ⃗q will\nrestore the state to s by construction. The number of weights loading times during ⃗q is ⌈M/M ′⌉, which meets the lower\nbound, and achieves the throughput upper bound c/⌈M/M ′⌉/tw. The warm-up phase can be ignored in the setting of an\ninﬁnite sequence. In summary, the diagonal block schedule is I/O optimal asymptotically.\nThe zig-zag block schedule is not optimal, as the peak memory consumption is not the same each time loading the weights.\nWhen computing the layers for the last token, the peak memory is scaled with s + n −1, while for the ﬁrst token, it is scaled\nwith s. In order to let the former ﬁt in M ′, the latter must be smaller than M ′. But the memory consumption change is\nlinear when generating the tokens, thus the average memory consumption for each weights loading can be pushed to at\nleast M ′/2. From this, the zig-zag block schedule can achieve the throughput at least c/⌈M/(M ′/2)⌉/tw which is 1/2 of\nthe throughput upper bound. In the inﬁnite sequence setting, this means the zig-zag block schedule can achieve an I/O\ncomplexity that is at most 2× optimal. Therefore, we have:\nTheorem 4.1. The I/O complexity of the zig-zag block schedule is within 2× of the optimal solution.\nA.3. Cost Model\nIn this section, we present the full cost model. Note that we use a single variable to represent constants like bandwidth and\nTFLOPS to simplify the formulation below. In real systems, these constants vary according to the total load. We handle such\ndynamics by using piece-wise functions and adding regularization terms. We carefully model the dynamics by depending\nonly on other constants (e.g., hidden size), so the optimization problem remains a linear programming problem with respect\nto policy variables.\nTable 8 and Table 9 give the meaning of constants used in the cost model.\nVar\nMeaning\nctog bdw\nCPU to GPU bandwidth\ngtoc bdw\nGPU to CPU bandwidth\ndtoc bdw\ndisk to CPU bandwidth\nctod bdw\nCPU to disk bandwidth\nmm flops\nGPU ﬂops per second for matrix multiplication\nbmm flops\nGPU ﬂops per second for batched matrix multiplication\ncpu flops\nCPU ﬂops per second\nwg\npercentage of weights on GPU\nwc\npercentage of weights on CPU\nwd\npercentage of weights on disk\ncg\npercentage of KV cache on GPU\ncc\npercentage of KV cache on CPU\ncd\npercentage of KV cache on disk\nhg\npercentage of activations on GPU\nhc\npercentage of activations on CPU\nhd\npercentage of activations on disk\nTable 9. For example, the layer could be at the tensor level to make squares extremely ﬁne-grained.\n18\n\n\n=== PDF page 19 ===\nThe object is to maximize throughput (token/s), which is equivalent to minimizing the reciprocal (s/token).
